Some facts about the optimality of the LSE in the Gaussian sequence model with convex constraint


Abstract
We consider a convex constrained Gaussian sequence model and characterize necessary and sufficient conditions for the least squares estimator (LSE) to be minimax optimal. For a closed convex set K ⊂ ℝ n K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n} we observe Y = μ + ξ Y=\mu+\xi for ξ ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( 0 , σ 2 ​ 𝕀 n ) \xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I}_{n}) and μ ∈ K \mu\in K and aim to estimate μ \mu . We characterize the worst case risk of the LSE in multiple ways by analyzing the behavior of the local Gaussian width on K K . We demonstrate that optimality is equivalent to a Lipschitz property of the local Gaussian width mapping. We also provide theoretical algorithms that search for the worst case risk. We then provide examples showing optimality or suboptimality of the LSE on various sets, including ℓ p \ell_{p} balls for p ∈ [ 1 , 2 ] p\in[1,2] , pyramids, solids of revolution, and multivariate isotonic regression, among others.



Akshay Prasadan and Matey Neykov

Department of Statistics & Data Science, Carnegie Mellon University

Department of Statistics and Data Science, Northwestern University

aprasada@andrew.cmu.edu
,
mneykov@northwestern.edu

1
Introduction

In this paper we focus on the Gaussian sequence model problem. Specifically, we observe a single observation
Y=\mu+\xi
, where
\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I}_{n})
is a multivariate Gaussian noise and
\mu\in K
where
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a known closed convex constraint. Our goal is to estimate the vector
\mu
taking into account the convex constraint, with the hope that our estimator will be optimal and computationally tractable. A popular estimator in this setting is the Least Squares Estimator (LSE) which essentially projects the observation
Y
onto the convex constraint. In detail, the LSE is given by

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}=\hat{\mu}(Y):=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\nu\in K}\|Y-\nu\|^{2},

(1.1)

where we abbreviated the Euclidean norm by
\|\cdot\|
. By definition, we have that
\hat{\mu}
is the Euclidean projection of
Y
onto the set
K
which is sometimes denoted by
\Pi_{K}Y
. The LSE is perhaps one of the most intuitive estimators for this problem (it is also the maximum likelihood estimator); in addition, the LSE can be solved for a variety of sets
K
as Euclidean projection is a well studied convex problem and there exist plenty of methods which can calculate
\hat{\mu}
. Unfortunately, it is known that there exist sets
K
for which the LSE is far from optimal in the worst case. Our goal in this paper is to give some insights into when is
\hat{\mu}
an “optimal” estimator. Here we will measure optimality with respect to the expected squared
\ell_{2}
loss. Taking a worst case perspective we would like to compare
\sup_{\mu\in K}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\|\hat{\mu}-\mu\|^{2}
to the minimax optimal rate, i.e., up to constants the expression
\inf_{\hat{\nu}}\sup_{\mu\in K}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\|\hat{\nu}(Y)-\mu\|^{2}
, with the infimum taken with respect to all measurable functions (i.e., estimators) of the data. Our primary technique will be analyzing the local geometry of
K
using a localized version of the Gaussian width as well as the metric entropy. We will give numerous necessary and sufficient conditions for LSE optimality, and then apply these results to a wide variety of examples and non-examples of LSE optimality.

1.1
Related Literature

Recently, the minimax rate in this problem was characterized in terms of the local geometry of the set
K

(
Neykov, 2022
)
. On the other hand,
Chatterjee (2014)
characterized the expression
\mathbb{E}\|\hat{\mu}-\mu\|^{2}
for any point
\mu\in K
. It may appear that the problem we posed above is nearly solved. The only thing one needs to do is to somehow add a
\sup_{\mu\in K}
in front of
Chatterjee (2014)
’s variational formula (see (
2.1
) below) and compare that to the minimax rate. However, this turns out to be more challenging than one may anticipate, as we hope to convince the reader in Section
2
.

While
Chatterjee (2014)
does not establish LSE optimality conditions in full generality, the work does demonstrate that the LSE satisfies an admissibility condition (see
Chatterjee (2014, Theorem 1.4)
,
Chen et al. (2017)
,
Kur et al. (2023)
). That is, up to some universal constant, for any arbitrary estimator
\tilde{\mu}
, there exists a
\mu\in K
for which the LSE has a lower risk than the arbitrary estimator up to that universal constant. This means the LSE is preferable for some region of the parameter space. Our paper on the other hand focuses on the worst case risk of the LSE.

The optimality of the LSE is well known for a wide class of examples. For isotonic regression,
Zhang (2002)
,
Bellec (2018)
demonstrate upper bounds on the LSE risk that match the minimax lower bound from
Bellec and Tsybakov (2015, Corollary 5)
. In multivariate isotonic regression,
Deng and Zhang (2020)
propose a block estimator that outperforms the LSE in certain cases.
Wei et al. (2020)
establishes LSE optimality for ellipsoid estimation problems under regularity assumptions that hold for Sobolev ellipsoids with smoothness parameter
\alpha>1/2
. Suboptimal examples can be found in
Zhang (2013)
;
Chatterjee (2014)
.

An example of our set-up is non-parametric regression with fixed design, i.e.,

K=\{(f(x_{1}),\ldots,f(x_{n}))\colon f\in\mathcal{F}\},

where the
x_{i}
are fixed points and
\mathcal{F}
is a convex class of functions.
Kur et al. (2020b)
give suboptimality results for the LSE in non-Donsker regimes, where the logarithm of the metric entropy has rate
\delta^{-\alpha}
for
\alpha>2
.
Han (2021)
further investigates the non-Donsker setting, and under a stronger assumption on the entropy of the function class, aims to close the gap between the upper and lower bounds on the LSE risk that derives from an entropy integral condition
(
Birgé and Massart, 1993
)
.
Kur et al. (2020a)
establishes suboptimality when the domain of functions in
\mathcal{F}
is a polytope of dimension at least 5, with applications to convex function classes with Lipschitz or boundedness constraints.
Kur et al. (2023)
, who also give a generalization of the admissibility result from
Chatterjee (2014)
, demonstrates that suboptimality emerges from the bias portion of the risk following a bias-variance decomposition.

1.2
Notation and Definitions

We define
[n]:=\{1,\dots,n\}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We write
a\lesssim b
if for some absolute constant
C>0
we have
a\leq Cb
, and similarly define
\gtrsim
. We write
a\asymp b
if
a\lesssim b
and
b\lesssim a
both hold, for possibly different constants. Unless otherwise specified, we operate on sets
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and use
\|\cdot\|_{2}
to denote the Euclidean
\ell_{2}
-norm on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, dropping the subscript when clear. For any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\varepsilon>0
, we define
B(\theta,\varepsilon)=\{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon\}
. We define
\mathbb{R}^{+}=(0,\infty)
. We write the
n\times n
identity matrix as
\mathbb{I}_{n}
. We denote by
d
the diameter of
K
, i.e.,
d:=\mathrm{diam}(K):=\sup\{\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{2}:\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in K\}
. We will define several quantities related to the Gaussian width and least squares estimator, usually denoted by adding subscripts to
\varepsilon
. For convenience, we summarize the notation in Table
1
.

Definition 1.1

(Packing Sets and Global Entropy)
.

An
\varepsilon
-packing of a (totally) bounded set
T\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{2}
is a set
\{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{M}\}\subset T
such that
\|\theta_{i}-\theta_{j}\|_{2}>\varepsilon
for all
i\neq j
. The
\varepsilon
-packing number
\mathcal{M}(\varepsilon,T)
is the largest possible cardinality of an
\varepsilon
-packing of
T
. The global entropy of
T
at distance
\varepsilon
is simply the number
\log\mathcal{M}(\varepsilon,T)
.

Definition 1.2

(Local Entropy)
.

Let
c^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
be a sufficiently large absolute constant.
1
1

1

We also reserve
c^{*}
to refer to this constant throughout this paper.
Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)=\sup_{\theta\in K}\mathcal{M}(\varepsilon/c^{\ast},B(\theta,\varepsilon)\cap K),

i.e., the largest
(\varepsilon/c^{\ast})
-packing of a set of the form
B(\theta,\varepsilon)\cap K
.
We refer to
\log\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)
as the local entropy of
K
. We drop the subscript
K
from
\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}
if the context is clear.

Definition 1.3

((Local) Gaussian Width)
.

The Gaussian width of a set
T\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is defined by
w(T)=\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in T}\langle x,t\rangle\right]
where
x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{n})
. Let
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and consider the set
B(\mu,\varepsilon)\cap K
. Then the local Gaussian width at
\mu
is the function defined as
w_{K,\mu}(\varepsilon)=w(B(\mu,\varepsilon)\cap K)
. When the set
K
is clear from the context, we will denote
w_{K,\mu}(\varepsilon)
with
w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)
.

Neykov (2022)
characterized up to absolute constant factors the minimax rate. It turns out to be
\inf_{\hat{\nu}}\sup_{\mu\in K}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\|\hat{\nu}(Y)-\mu\|_{2}^{2}\asymp\varepsilon^{*2}\wedge d^{2}
, where

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{*}:=\sup\{\varepsilon:\varepsilon^{2}/\sigma^{2}\leq\log\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)\}.

(1.2)

In fact, we can show
\varepsilon^{\ast}\lesssim d
. To see this, take
\varepsilon\geq(c^{\ast}+\kappa)d
for any
\kappa>0
. Then
\log\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)=0
, since for any
\mu\in K
, if we pack
K\cap B(\mu,(c^{\ast}+\kappa)d)=K
using points of distance at least
\frac{c^{\ast}+\kappa}{c^{\ast}}d>d
apart, there can only be one point in the packing. This implies by (
1.2
) that
\varepsilon^{\ast}\leq\varepsilon
for all
\varepsilon\geq(c^{\ast}+\kappa)d
, implying
\varepsilon^{\ast}\lesssim(c^{\ast}+\kappa)d
. Hence
\varepsilon^{\ast}\wedge d\asymp\varepsilon^{\ast}
.

We will now give a quick universal lower bound on the minimax rate which will be useful later on. We give a proof using the definition of
\varepsilon^{\ast}
but it can also be deduced from a two point lower bound argument. In addition, we show a different lower bound on the rate involving the Gaussian width of the set
K
in the appendix (see Lemma
B.1
).

Lemma 1.4

(Minimax Rate Bound)
.

The minimax rate
\varepsilon^{\ast}
satisfies
\varepsilon^{\ast}\gtrsim\sigma\wedge d
.

Another useful property is invariance of the minimax rate to the constants used inside or outside the local metric entropy term, as the following lemma shows. The proof relies on the fact that
\varepsilon\mapsto\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}_{K}(\varepsilon)
is non-increasing for convex sets
K
(or more generally, star-shaped ones), as established in
Neykov (2022, Lemma II.8)
.

Lemma 1.5

(Equivalent Forms of Information Theoretic Lower Bound)
.

Define
\varepsilon^{{\dagger}}=\sup\{\varepsilon>0:\varepsilon^{2}/\sigma^{2}\leq C_{1}\log\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(C_{2}\varepsilon)\}
for any fixed
C_{1},C_{2}>0
. Then we have
\varepsilon^{\ast}\asymp\varepsilon^{\dagger}
, where
\varepsilon^{\ast}=\sup\{\varepsilon:\varepsilon^{2}/\sigma^{2}\leq\log\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)\}
.

1.3
Organization

In Section
2
, we begin by introducing a variational quantity from
Chatterjee (2014)
related to the least squares error. We use the relationship between the two to then derive bounds on the worst case LSE error. We then characterize a sufficient condition for the LSE to be minimax optimal. Then we derive numerous other variational formulas that let us bound the worst case LSE rate.

Section
3
is split into two parts. First, we illustrate numerous examples where the LSE is minimax optimal, or nearly so. This includes isotonic regression in both univariate and multivariate settings, hyper-rectangles, subspaces, and
\ell_{p}
balls for
p\in\{1,2\}
. We then show examples where the LSE is suboptimal, including pyramids, multivariate isotonic regression when the noise is too large, solids of revolution, ellipsoids, and
\ell_{p}
balls for
p\in(1,2)
.

In Appendix
A
, we develop two theoretical algorithms that aim to find this worst case LSE rate provided the set
K
is bounded, using some of the variational quantities we defined. The remaining appendices contain the proofs of our main results and examples.

2
Main Results

According to
Chatterjee (2014, Theorem 1.1)
the maximizer of

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mu,w}(\sigma):=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\varepsilon}[\sigma w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)-\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}]

(2.1)

is very related (i.e., in some sense it controls) the risk
\mathbb{E}\|\hat{\mu}-\mu\|^{2}
where
\hat{\mu}=\hat{\mu}(Y)
is the LSE defined in (
1.1
).
This fact can also be seen in the following lemma, which follows a similar logic to
Chatterjee (2014, Corollary 1.2)
. For completeness, we give the full proof in the appendix.

Lemma 2.1
.

If
\varepsilon_{\mu,w}(\sigma)\geq C\sigma
, then
\mathbb{E}\|\hat{\mu}-\mu\|^{2}\asymp\varepsilon_{\mu,w}^{2}
. On the other hand, if
\varepsilon_{\mu,w}(\sigma)<C\sigma
, then
\mathbb{E}\|\hat{\mu}-\mu\|^{2}\lesssim\sigma^{2}
. Here
\gtrsim,\asymp
and
\lesssim
hide universal constants and
C>0
is another universal constant (some values of the constants are calculated in the proof of the lemma).

We now define
\varepsilon_{K,w}(\sigma):=\sup_{\mu\in K}\varepsilon_{\mu,w}(\sigma)
. When the context is clear, we drop the
\sigma
from
\varepsilon_{\mu,w}
or
\varepsilon_{K,w}
. The following lemmas give some additional properties of
\varepsilon_{K,w}(\sigma)
that we use in Section
A
where we give algorithms to find the worst case LSE rate.

Lemma 2.2
.

\varepsilon_{K,w}(\sigma)\leq d
where
d
is the diameter of
K
.

Proof.

Fix any
\mu\in K
, and let
\delta>d
. Note that
w_{\mu}(\delta)=w_{\mu}(d)=w(K)
since
B(\mu,d)\cap K=B(\mu,\delta)\cap K=K
. Thus,

\sigma w_{\mu}(\delta)-\delta^{2}/2<\sigma w_{\mu}(d)-d^{2}/2.

Hence
\varepsilon_{\mu,w}(\sigma)=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\varepsilon}[\sigma w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)-\varepsilon^{2}/2]\leq d
. Since this holds for all
\mu\in K
, we have
\varepsilon_{K,w}(\sigma)\leq d
.
∎

Lemma 2.3
.

The map
\sigma\mapsto\varepsilon_{K,w}(\sigma)
is non-decreasing on
[0,\infty)
. If
c\geq 1
, then
\varepsilon_{K,w}(\sigma)\leq\varepsilon_{K,w}(c\sigma)\leq c\varepsilon_{K,w}(\sigma)
. If
0\leq c<1
, then
c\varepsilon_{K,w}(\sigma)\leq\varepsilon_{K,w}(c\sigma)\leq\varepsilon_{K,w}(\sigma)
.

2.1
Sufficient conditions on the worst case performance of the LSE

We now consider the worst case risk of the LSE estimator and prove some simple bounds. Let

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}^{2}(\sigma):=\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}^{2}:=\sup_{\mu\in K}\mathbb{E}\|\hat{\mu}-\mu\|^{2}

denote the worst case risk for the LSE estimator
\hat{\mu}
. Since
\hat{\mu}\in K
, clearly
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}\leq\mathrm{diam}(K)=:d
. We will now establish the following upper bound on
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}
.

Table 2:
Controlling the worst case LSE rate with different choices of
\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma)
.

Definition of
\overline{\varepsilon}=\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma)

Usage

1

\begin{aligned} &\sup_{\varepsilon}\{\varepsilon:\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2\sigma}\leq\sup_{\mu\in K}w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)\}\end{aligned}

Prop.
2.4

2

\begin{aligned} &\sup_{\varepsilon}\bigg\{\varepsilon:\sup_{\mu\in K}\big[w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)-\inf_{\nu\in B(\mu,\varepsilon)\cap K}w_{\nu}(\tfrac{\varepsilon}{c})\big]\geq\left(4+\tfrac{4}{c}\right)\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2\sigma}\bigg\}\end{aligned}

Thm.
2.14

3

\begin{aligned} &\sup_{\varepsilon}\Big\{\varepsilon:\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in K\\
\|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}\|\leq 2\varepsilon\end{subarray}}w_{\nu_{1}}(\tfrac{\varepsilon}{c^{\ast}})-w_{\nu_{2}}(\tfrac{\varepsilon}{c^{\ast}})-\tfrac{C\varepsilon^{2}}{2\sigma}\\
&\qquad\qquad+\tfrac{L}{c^{\ast}}\cdot\varepsilon\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)}\geq 0\Big\}\end{aligned}

Thm.
2.15

4

\begin{aligned} &\sup_{\varepsilon}\Big\{\varepsilon:\sup_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in K}w_{\nu_{1}}(\tfrac{\varepsilon}{c^{\ast}})-w_{\nu_{2}}(\tfrac{\varepsilon}{c^{\ast}})-\frac{C\varepsilon\|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}\|}{\sigma}\\
&\qquad\qquad+\tfrac{L}{c^{\ast}}\|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}\|\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)}\geq 0\Big\}\end{aligned}

Thm.
2.18

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
\overline{\varepsilon}:=\sup_{\varepsilon}\{\varepsilon^{2}/(2\sigma)\leq\sup_{\mu\in K}w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)\}
. Then
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}\lesssim\overline{\varepsilon}\wedge d
.

Proof.

Since
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}\lesssim d
always holds, it suffices to show
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}\lesssim\overline{\varepsilon}
. Recall we set
\varepsilon_{K,w}=\sup_{\mu\in K}\varepsilon_{\mu,w}
, where
\varepsilon_{\mu,w}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\varepsilon}\sigma w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)-\varepsilon^{2}/2
. Observe that for every
\mu\in K
, we have
\sigma w_{\mu}(2\overline{\varepsilon})-2\overline{\varepsilon}^{2}<0
, and thus by
Chatterjee (2014, Proposition 1.3)
we have
2\overline{\varepsilon}>\varepsilon_{K,w}
. We now relate
\varepsilon_{K,w}
to
{\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}}
.

Case 1:
Suppose
\varepsilon_{K,w}\gtrsim\sigma
. Now pick
\tilde{\mu}
that maximizes
\mathbb{E}\|\hat{\mu}-\tilde{\mu}\|^{2}
. This leads to two subcases.

Case 1(a):
Suppose
\varepsilon_{\tilde{\mu},w}\gtrsim\sigma
. Then by definition of
\tilde{\mu}
and
\varepsilon_{K,w}
along with Lemma
2.1
, we have

\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\|\hat{\mu}-\tilde{\mu}\|^{2}\asymp\varepsilon_{\tilde{\mu},w}^{2}\lesssim\varepsilon_{K,w}^{2}.

But we showed
\varepsilon_{K,w}\lesssim\overline{\varepsilon}
, so we conclude
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}\lesssim\overline{\varepsilon}
as claimed.

Case 1(b):
Suppose
\varepsilon_{\tilde{\mu},w}\lesssim\sigma
. Then by Lemma
2.1
and our Case 1 assumption,

\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\|\hat{\mu}-\tilde{\mu}\|^{2}\lesssim\sigma^{2}\lesssim\varepsilon_{K,w}^{2}\lesssim 4\overline{\varepsilon}^{2}.

This concludes Case 1.

Case 2:
Suppose
\varepsilon_{K,w}\lesssim\sigma
. This means for any
\mu\in K
,
\varepsilon_{\mu,w}\lesssim\sigma
which in turn implies for all
\mu
that
\mathbb{E}\|\hat{\mu}-\mu\|\lesssim\sigma
by Lemma
2.1
. Hence
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}\lesssim\sigma
.

Case 2(a):
Suppose furthermore that
d\geq\frac{2\sigma(n+1)}{n\sqrt{2\pi}}
. We claim this implies
\overline{\varepsilon}\gtrsim\sigma
which would prove
\overline{\varepsilon}\gtrsim\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}
since
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}\lesssim\sigma
in Case 2. To see that
\overline{\varepsilon}\gtrsim\sigma
, notice that
K
contains a diameter of length
2\sigma/\kappa
(for a sufficiently large absolute constant
\kappa
). Thus by
Vershynin (2018, Proposition 7.5.2(vi))
,
\sup_{\mu\in K}w_{\mu}(\sigma/\kappa)\geq 2\sigma/(\kappa\sqrt{2\pi})
. Now for sufficiently large
\kappa
we have
\sigma/(2\kappa^{2})\leq 2\sigma/(\kappa\sqrt{2\pi})
, so that
\sup_{\mu\in K}w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)\geq\varepsilon^{2}/2\sigma
holds for
\varepsilon=\sigma/\kappa
. But then
\overline{\varepsilon}\geq\sigma/\kappa
by definition as a supremum of such
\varepsilon
. Hence
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}\lesssim\sigma\lesssim\overline{\varepsilon}
as desired.

Case 2(b):
Suppose
d\leq\frac{2\sigma(n+1)}{n\sqrt{2\pi}}
. By Jung’s Theorem and
Vershynin (2018, Proposition 7.5.2(vi))
, there exists
\mu\in K
such that

w_{\mu}\left(\sqrt{\tfrac{n}{2(n+1)}}d\right)=w(K)\geq\tfrac{d}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\geq\tfrac{d}{2\sqrt{\pi}}.

Our assumption on
d
implies
\frac{nd^{2}}{2(n+1)(2\sigma)}\leq\tfrac{d}{2\sqrt{2\pi}}
which in turn implies that
w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)\geq\varepsilon^{2}/(2\sigma)
for
\varepsilon=\sqrt{\frac{nd^{2}}{2(n+1)}}\gtrsim d
. Therefore,
\overline{\varepsilon}\gtrsim d
due to its definition as a supremum, and since
d\lesssim\overline{\varepsilon}\wedge d\leq d
, we have
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}\lesssim d\asymp\overline{\varepsilon}\wedge d
.
∎

Remark 2.5
.

It should be noted that Proposition
2.4
is simply an upper bound on the rate of the LSE. We will later see an example with hyper-rectangles (Section
3.1.3
) where this upper bound is very suboptimal.

Corollary 2.6
.

Suppose that
\sup_{\mu\in K}w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)/\varepsilon\lesssim\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(c\varepsilon)}
for all
\varepsilon\leq d
. Then the LSE is minimax optimal for all
\sigma
. For (centrally) symmetric sets it suffices to examine the
0
point only (both for the maximal local Gaussian width and for the local entropy).

Proof.

For each
\mu
the map
\varepsilon\mapsto\tfrac{w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}
is non-increasing, hence
\varepsilon\mapsto\sup_{\mu\in K}\tfrac{w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}
is also non-increasing. To see this, note that for
\varepsilon<\delta
, since
w_{\mu}(0)=0
, we have

\displaystyle w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)\geq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\cdot w_{\mu}(\delta)+(1-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{\delta})\cdot w_{\mu}(0)

by concavity of
\varepsilon\mapsto w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)

(
Chatterjee, 2014
, Proof of Theorem 1.1)
, which implies
\tfrac{w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}\geq\tfrac{w_{\mu}(\delta)}{\delta}.

Recall Proposition
2.4
and the definition of
\overline{\varepsilon}
and note that
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}\lesssim\overline{\varepsilon}\wedge d\leq d
. This means for some
c^{\prime}>0
,
c^{\prime}\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}\leq\overline{\varepsilon}
. Pick some
c^{\prime\prime}\in(0,1)
so that
c^{\prime}\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}\leq c^{\prime\prime}\overline{\varepsilon}
. By definition,
c^{\prime\prime}\overline{\varepsilon}/(2\sigma)\leq\sup_{\mu\in K}w_{\mu}(c^{\prime\prime}\overline{\varepsilon})/(c^{\prime\prime}\overline{\varepsilon})
. Using these facts and the aforementioned non-increasing property of
\varepsilon\mapsto\sup_{\mu\in K}\tfrac{w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{c^{\prime}\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}}{2\sigma}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c^{\prime\prime}\overline{\varepsilon}}{2\sigma}\leq\frac{\sup_{\mu\in K}w_{\mu}(c^{\prime\prime}\overline{\varepsilon})}{c^{\prime\prime}\overline{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sup_{\mu\in K}w_{\mu}(c^{\prime}\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}})}{c^{\prime}\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}}.

If
c^{\prime}>1
, then
\sup_{\mu\in K}\frac{w_{\mu}(c^{\prime}\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}})}{c^{\prime}\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}}\leq\sup_{\mu\in K}\frac{w_{\mu}(\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}})}{\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}}
again by the non-increasing property so that
\frac{\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}}{2\sigma}\lesssim\sup_{\mu\in K}\frac{w_{\mu}(\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}})}{\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}}
. Then using our assumption,
\sup_{\mu\in K}\frac{w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}\lesssim\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(c\varepsilon)}
holds for
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}
since
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}\leq d
. Hence
\frac{\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\lesssim\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(c\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}})
, which by Lemma
1.5
implies
\varepsilon^{\ast}\gtrsim\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}
, i.e., the LSE is minimax optimal.

On the other hand, if
c^{\prime}\leq 1
, then we have
\sup_{\mu\in K}w_{\mu}(c^{\prime}\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}})\leq\sup_{\mu\in K}w_{\mu}(\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}})
, therefore

\displaystyle\frac{c^{\prime}\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}}{2\sigma}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sup_{\mu\in K}w_{\mu}(c^{\prime}\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}})}{c^{\prime}\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}}\leq\frac{\sup_{\mu\in K}w_{\mu}(\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}})}{c^{\prime}\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(c\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}})}.

Once more we have
\varepsilon^{\ast}\gtrsim\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}
.

To see the last implication, let
K
be a centrally symmetric set and suppose we have
w_{0}(\varepsilon)/\varepsilon\lesssim\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}(c\varepsilon/c^{\ast},B(0,c\varepsilon)\cap K)}
for all
\varepsilon\leq d
. Pick any
\mu\in K
. Note that

\displaystyle\alpha[B(\nu,\varepsilon)\cap K]+(1-\alpha)[B(\mu,\varepsilon)\cap K]

\displaystyle\subseteq B(\alpha\nu+(1-\alpha)\mu,\varepsilon)\cap K.

Take
\nu=-\mu
and
\alpha=1/2
, so that
(1/2)[B(-\mu,\varepsilon)\cap K]+(1/2)[B(\mu,\varepsilon)\cap K]\subseteq B(0,\varepsilon)\cap K
. The width of
B(-\mu,\varepsilon)\cap K
and
B(\mu,\varepsilon)\cap K
are the same by central symmetry. Hence

\displaystyle w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)=w(B(\mu,\varepsilon)\cap K)\leq w(B(0,\varepsilon)\cap K)=w_{0}(\varepsilon).

We then consider the local entropy. Let
\nu\in K
be arbitrary, and let
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{M}
be a maximal
c\varepsilon/c^{*}
packing of
B(\nu,c\varepsilon)\cap K
. By central symmetry, the points
(\theta_{i}-\nu)/2
form a
c\varepsilon/(2c^{*})
packing of
B(0,c\varepsilon/2)\cap K
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(c\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\nu\in K}\log\mathcal{M}(c\varepsilon/c^{\ast},B(0,c\varepsilon)\cap K)

\displaystyle\leq\log\mathcal{M}(c\varepsilon/(2c^{*}),B(0,c\varepsilon/2)\cap K)

\displaystyle\leq\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(c\varepsilon/2).

Thus,
\sup_{\mu\in K}w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)/\varepsilon\leq\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(c\varepsilon/2)}
. Hence our hypothesis need only check the zero point of a centrally symmetric set.
∎

Remark 2.7
.

One may conjecture that the condition in Corollary
2.6
is also necessary. We will see a counterexample in Section
3.1.3
with hyper-rectangles. We also consider ellipsoids and derive a necessary condition similar in spirit to the corollary.

Remark 2.8
.

Another quick corollary to Proposition
2.4
is that
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}\lesssim\sqrt{\sigma w(K)}
by trivially bounding
w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)\leq w(K)
. This bound is achievable for some sets
K
; for an example, see Section
3.2.5
.

Proposition
2.4
can be extended to a slightly more general upper bound. Suppose we are interested in the worst case risk over a convex subset
K^{\prime}\subseteq K
. In other words let

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{K^{\prime};K,\mathrm{LS}}^{2}:=\sup_{\mu\in K^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}\|\hat{\mu}-\mu\|^{2}

(2.2)

denote the worst case risk for the LSE estimator
\hat{\mu}
. We will now establish the following:

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
\overline{\varepsilon}_{K^{\prime};K}:=\sup_{\varepsilon}\{\varepsilon^{2}/(2\sigma)\leq\sup_{\mu\in K^{\prime}}w_{K,\mu}(\varepsilon)\}
. Then
\varepsilon_{K^{\prime};K,\mathrm{LS}}\lesssim\overline{\varepsilon}_{K^{\prime};K}
.

Since the proof of Proposition
2.9
is almost identical to that of Proposition
2.4
, we defer it to the appendix. So far we saw some sufficient conditions for the worst-case performance of the LSE. We now derive a similar version of the celebrated result of
Birgé and Massart (1993)
using Proposition
2.4
. For a related result, see
Wainwright (2019, Corollary 13.7)
.

Corollary 2.10
.

Let
\overline{\varepsilon}
be as defined in Proposition
2.4
, and let
c^{\prime}
be some absolute constant. Suppose
\sigma=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}
. If
\varepsilon
is such that
\int_{(c^{\prime}/16)\varepsilon^{2}}^{2\varepsilon}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}(t,K)}\mathrm{d}t\lesssim\sqrt{n}\varepsilon^{2}
, then
\varepsilon\gtrsim\overline{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

Using Dudley’s entropy bound (see
Wainwright (2019, Theorem 5.22)
), for any fixed
c
we have

\displaystyle w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)\leq 2\sqrt{n}c^{2}\varepsilon^{2}+C\int_{c^{2}c^{\prime}\varepsilon^{2}}^{2\varepsilon}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}(t,B(\mu,\varepsilon)\cap K)}\mathrm{d}t,

where
c^{\prime},C
are absolute constants. We can now bound

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{M}(t,B(\mu,\varepsilon)\cap K)\leq\log\mathcal{M}(t,K),

which makes the bound independent of
\mu
. Let
c=1/4
. Then

\displaystyle\sigma\sup_{\mu}w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)\leq\varepsilon^{2}/8+C/\sqrt{n}\int_{(c^{\prime}/16)\varepsilon^{2}}^{2\varepsilon}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}(t,K)}\mathrm{d}t,

and so as long as
\int_{(c^{\prime}/16)\varepsilon^{2}}^{2\varepsilon}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}(t,K)}\mathrm{d}t\lesssim\sqrt{n}\varepsilon^{2}
, we have
\sigma\sup_{\mu}w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)\leq C^{\prime}\varepsilon^{2}
for some absolute constant
C^{\prime}>1
. This implies
\varepsilon\gtrsim\overline{\varepsilon}
. To see this, first observe that
\varepsilon\mapsto\frac{\sup_{\mu}w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}
is non-increasing. Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{\sup_{\mu}w_{\mu}(2C^{\prime}\varepsilon)}{2C^{\prime}\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sup_{\mu}w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}\leq\frac{C^{\prime}\varepsilon}{\sigma}.

Rearranging,
\sup_{\mu}w_{\mu}(2C^{\prime}\varepsilon)\leq\frac{(2C^{\prime}\varepsilon)^{2}}{2\sigma}
, but by definition of
\overline{\varepsilon}
, this implies
\overline{\varepsilon}\leq 2C^{\prime}\varepsilon
.
∎

The next set of results bound the LSE with a geometric average of the minimax rate and a trivial estimator up to some logarithmic factors. Define
\underline{\varepsilon}=\sup_{\varepsilon}\{\varepsilon^{2}/(2\sigma)\leq\frac{1}{2}\sup_{\delta\leq\varepsilon}\tfrac{\delta}{c^{\ast}}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\delta)}\}
. We first compare
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}
to
\underline{\varepsilon}
up to log factors (Theorem
2.11
) and then derive the geometric average result (Corollary
2.12
). Remark
2.13
examines the sharpness of the bound and connects the results to a Donsker-regime assumption.

Theorem 2.11
.

Let
C_{n}=4C\bigg({1+\log_{c^{\ast}}\sqrt{2\pi n}}\bigg)^{3/2}
, where
C>1
and
c^{\ast}
are sufficiently large absolute constants (and
c^{\ast}
is the constant from the definition of local entropy). Then
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}\lesssim(4C_{n}\underline{\varepsilon})\wedge d
.

Corollary 2.12
.

The following inequality always holds for the LSE:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}\lesssim\sqrt{\sigma}C_{n}(\sqrt{\varepsilon^{*}}\sqrt[4]{n}).

Proof.

Let
\delta_{\kappa}\leq\underline{\varepsilon}
be such that
\delta_{\kappa}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\delta_{\kappa})}\geq\sup_{\delta\leq\underline{\varepsilon}}\delta\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\delta)}-\kappa
.

Case 1:
\delta_{\kappa}>\varepsilon^{*}
. Then using that
\delta_{\kappa}\leq\underline{\varepsilon}
, the non-decreasing property of
\varepsilon\mapsto\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)
, and the definition of
\varepsilon^{\ast}
, we obtain

\delta_{\kappa}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\delta_{\kappa})}\leq\underline{\varepsilon}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon^{*})}\asymp\frac{\underline{\varepsilon}\varepsilon^{*}}{\sigma}.

Using the definition of
\underline{\varepsilon}
, we can show
\underline{\varepsilon}\lesssim\varepsilon^{\ast}
since

\displaystyle\frac{\underline{\varepsilon}^{2}}{2\sigma}\leq\frac{1}{2c^{\ast}}\left(\delta_{\kappa}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\delta_{\kappa})}+\kappa\right)\lesssim\frac{\underline{\varepsilon}\varepsilon^{\ast}}{\sigma}.

By Theorem
2.11
, we obtain

\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}\lesssim C_{n}\underline{\varepsilon}\lesssim C_{n}\varepsilon^{*}=C_{n}\sqrt{\varepsilon^{\ast}}\sqrt{\varepsilon^{\ast}}\lesssim C_{n}\sqrt{\varepsilon^{*}}\sqrt[4]{n}\sqrt{\sigma},

where we use the fact that
\varepsilon^{\ast}\leq\sqrt{n}\sigma
(which follows since the minimax estimator is at least better than using
Y
as an estimator of
\mu
, and this estimator has error rate
n\sigma^{2}
).

Case 2:
\delta_{\kappa}\leq\varepsilon^{*}
. Then we have
\delta_{\kappa}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\delta_{\kappa})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{*}\sqrt{n}
. Here we use the fact that
\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\delta_{\kappa})\lesssim n
. To see this, note that
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\delta_{\kappa})\leq\mathcal{M}(\delta_{\kappa}/c^{\ast},B(\theta,\delta_{\kappa}))\lesssim(1+{c^{\ast}})^{n}
using the well-known metric entropy of a scaled
\ell_{2}
-ball. Again using
\underline{\varepsilon}^{2}\lesssim\sigma(\delta_{k}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\delta_{\kappa})}+\kappa)
, we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{n}\underline{\varepsilon}\lesssim C_{n}\sqrt{\sigma\cdot\left(\delta_{k}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\delta_{\kappa})}+\kappa\right)}

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{n}\sqrt{\varepsilon^{*}}\sqrt[4]{n}\sqrt{\sigma}.

∎

Remark 2.13
.

We will see later that without further assumptions on
K
this bound is sharp up to the logarithmic factors, i.e., there exist sets
K
for which the bound is met with equality dropping the
\log
factors and constant terms (Section
3.2.5
). For now, it suffices to say that the bound is sharp when
\sigma\ll 1/\sqrt{n}
, and
K
is the unit
\ell_{2}
ball.
2
2

2

The minimax rate on the squared level for the unit
\ell_{2}
ball is given by
\min(1,n\sigma^{2})

(see
Zhang, 2013
, e.g.)
.

Note that the rate
\sqrt{\varepsilon^{*}}\sqrt{\sigma}\sqrt[4]{n}
is the geometric mean of the optimal rate, and a trivial rate (which is achieved by using the observation
Y
as the estimator of the mean). So the rate of the LSE is always not worse than this geometric mean. Furthermore, when
\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\delta)\asymp\delta^{-\alpha}
for some
\alpha<2
, the supremum satisfies
\sup_{\delta\leq\underline{\varepsilon}}\delta\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\delta)}\asymp(\varepsilon/c^{\ast})\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)
. Hence,upon equating this to
\varepsilon^{2}/(2\sigma)
, we realize that the LSE is minimax optimal up to logarithmic factors for all
\sigma
. This latter assumption is known as the “Donsker regime,” although it is typically assumed that the global entropy scales like
\delta^{-\alpha}
, a stronger requirement than the same assumption on the local entropy (see, e.g., Lemma
3.1
).

2.2
Characterizations of the worst case rate of the LSE

We will now see a series of results which attempt to characterize (up to constants) the worst case risk of the LSE. In the proof of these and several later results, we repeatedly use
Chatterjee (2014, Proposition 1.3)
to bound
\varepsilon_{\mu,w}(\sigma)
. That proposition notes that
\varepsilon\mapsto w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)-\varepsilon^{2}/(2\sigma)
is a strictly concave mapping, and as a consequence, if
\alpha\geq\beta>0
and

w_{\mu}(\alpha)-\alpha^{2}/(2\sigma)\geq w_{\mu}(\beta)-\beta^{2}/(2\sigma),

then
\varepsilon_{\mu,w}(\sigma)\geq\beta
. If on the other hand
w_{\mu}(\alpha)-\alpha^{2}/(2\sigma)\leq w_{\mu}(\beta)-\beta^{2}/(2\sigma)
, then
\varepsilon_{\mu,w}(\sigma)\leq\alpha
. Choosing
\mu\in K
and
\alpha,\beta>0
appropriately will give us the desired bounds on
\varepsilon_{\mu,w}(\sigma)
, and therefore
\varepsilon_{K,w}(\sigma)
and
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}
with the help of Lemma
2.1
.

We proceed to our first proper characterization, which we use in Lemma
3.16
to prove suboptimality of the LSE for
\ell_{p}
balls with
p\in(1,2)
.

Theorem 2.14
.

Let
\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma)
be defined as

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon}\bigg\{\varepsilon:\sup_{\mu\in K}\bigg[w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)-\inf_{\nu\in B(\mu,\varepsilon)\cap K}w_{\nu}(\tfrac{\varepsilon}{c})\bigg]\geq\left(4+\tfrac{4}{c}\right)\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2\sigma}\bigg\},

where
c
is some absolute constant. Set
\sigma^{\prime}=4c\sigma/(c-1)
. Then if
\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma)\gtrsim\sigma
for a sufficiently large constant, we have
\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma)/c\lesssim{\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}}(\sigma)\lesssim\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma^{\prime})
; if
\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma^{\prime})\lesssim\sigma
, we have
{\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}}(\sigma)\asymp\sigma\wedge d
.

A similar result to the one above is given by the next theorem. We later apply it to demonstrate LSE optimality for subspaces (Section
3.1.4
) and suboptimality for pyramids (Lemma
3.11
).

Theorem 2.15
.

Define
\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma)
by

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon}\bigg\{\varepsilon:

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in K\\
\|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}\|\leq 2\varepsilon\end{subarray}}w_{\nu_{1}}(\tfrac{\varepsilon}{c^{\ast}})-w_{\nu_{2}}(\tfrac{\varepsilon}{c^{\ast}})-\tfrac{C\varepsilon^{2}}{2\sigma}

\displaystyle+\tfrac{L}{c^{\ast}}\cdot\varepsilon\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)}\geq 0\bigg\}

where
C=2[(2+1/c^{\ast})^{2}-1/{c^{\ast}}^{2}]=8+8/c^{\ast}
for some
c^{\ast}>1
, and
L
is an absolute constant. Set
\sigma^{\prime}=\tfrac{C\sigma}{1-1/{c^{\ast}}^{2}}
.
If
\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma)\gtrsim\sigma
for a sufficiently large constant, then
\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma)/c^{\ast}\lesssim{\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}}(\sigma)\leq\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma^{\prime})
. If
\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma^{\prime})\lesssim\sigma
, then
{\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}}(\sigma)\asymp\sigma\wedge d
.

To prove this theorem, we additionally define

\displaystyle\underline{\varepsilon}^{\ast}=\sup\{\varepsilon>0:C^{2}\varepsilon^{2}/(4\sigma^{2})\leq(L/c^{\ast})^{2}\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)\}.

(2.3)

Luckily up to constants this is the information theoretic lower bound defined in (
1.2
), as we proved in Lemma
1.5
.

Remark 2.16
.

We have
\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma)\geq\underline{\varepsilon}^{\ast}\asymp\varepsilon^{\ast}
. To see this, pick any
\varepsilon\leq\underline{\varepsilon}^{\ast}
. By definition of
\underline{\varepsilon}^{\ast}
, we have
L/c^{\ast}\cdot\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)}-C\varepsilon/2\sigma>0
, and by choosing
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}
appropriately, the expression we take the supremum over in
\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma)
is non-negative and
\varepsilon
satisfies the given condition. By definition of
\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma)
as a supremum,
\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma)\geq\varepsilon
. Thus for any such
\varepsilon\leq\underline{\varepsilon}^{\ast}
, we can show that
\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma)\geq\varepsilon
, which implies
\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma)\geq\underline{\varepsilon}^{\ast}
. Then we invoke Lemma
1.5
.

In our next set of results, we analyze the Lipschitz constant of the map
\nu\mapsto w_{\nu}(\varepsilon)
over
K
, noting that this map is always Lipschitz (Remark
2.17
). It turns out this Lipschitz constant controls the worst case LSE rate
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}
(Theorem
2.18
) and thus yields an equivalent condition for LSE optimality (Corollary
2.19
). We conclude the section by observing some easier ways to prove this map is Lipschitz (Remark
2.20
). We apply Theorem
2.18
in our solids of revolution suboptimality result (Lemma
3.13
), and both this theorem and Corollary
2.19
trivially yield an optimality result for subspaces (Section
3.1.4
).

Remark 2.17
.

The mapping
\nu\mapsto w_{\nu}(\varepsilon)
is Lipschitz over
K
.

Proof.

Suppose for a fixed Gaussian vector
\xi
,
x_{\xi}
achieves the max
\langle\xi,x\rangle
over
x\in B(\mu,\varepsilon)\cap K
(if such a vector does not exist, i.e., the maximum is unattainable, the same reasoning works using a limiting sequence argument). Then the vector

\displaystyle y_{\xi}^{\prime}=\frac{\varepsilon}{\|\mu-\nu\|+\varepsilon}\cdot x_{\xi}+\frac{\|\mu-\nu\|}{\|\mu-\nu\|+\varepsilon}\cdot\nu

belongs to
B(\nu,\varepsilon)\cap K
since
y_{\xi}^{\prime}
is a convex combination of points in
K
and
\|y_{\xi}^{\prime}-\nu\|=\tfrac{\varepsilon}{\|\mu-\nu\|+\varepsilon}\cdot\|x_{\xi}-\nu\|\leq\varepsilon
. Using that
w_{\nu}(\varepsilon)\geq\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\langle\xi,y_{\xi}^{\prime}\rangle
and expanding the definition of
y_{\xi}^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)-w_{\nu}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\langle\xi,x_{\xi}\rangle-\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\langle\xi,y_{\xi}^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|\mu-\nu\|}{\|\mu-\nu\|+\varepsilon}(\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\langle\xi,x_{\xi}\rangle-\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\langle\xi,\nu\rangle}_{=0})

\displaystyle=\frac{\|\mu-\nu\|\cdot w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)}{\|\mu-\nu\|+\varepsilon}\leq\frac{\|\mu-\nu\|w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}.

Now, take
\alpha\mu+(1-\alpha)\nu
for
\alpha\in(0,1)
. We have by concavity of
\nu\mapsto w_{\nu}(\varepsilon)
(Lemma
C.2
) and the penultimate inequality above that

\displaystyle\alpha(w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)-w_{\nu}(\varepsilon))

\displaystyle\leq w_{\alpha\mu+(1-\alpha)\nu}(\varepsilon)-w_{\nu}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\|\mu-\nu\|\cdot\frac{w_{\alpha\mu+(1-\alpha)\nu}(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}.

Thus dividing by
\alpha
and taking
\alpha\rightarrow 0
(and noting that by its Lipschitz condition, the map
\alpha\rightarrow w_{\alpha\mu+(1-\alpha)\nu}(\varepsilon)
is continuous) shows that

\displaystyle w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)-w_{\nu}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|\mu-\nu\|w_{\nu}(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}.

Using the symmetry in
\mu
and
\nu
, we conclude

\displaystyle|w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)-w_{\nu}(\varepsilon)|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mu-\nu\|\cdot\frac{w_{\nu}(\varepsilon)\wedge w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{n}\|\mu-\nu\|.

(2.4)

The final inequality used
Vershynin (2018, Proposition 7.5.2(vi))

∎

Equipped with this fact, we now define a quantity
\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma)
which encodes the tightest such Lipschitz constant of our map, and then relate it to
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}(\sigma)
. The proof will rely on the concavity of
\nu\mapsto w_{\nu}(\varepsilon)
over
K
(Lemma
C.2
).

Theorem 2.18
.

Define
\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma)
by

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon}\bigg\{\varepsilon:

\displaystyle\sup_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in K}w_{\nu_{1}}(\varepsilon/c^{\ast})-w_{\nu_{2}}(\varepsilon/c^{\ast})-\frac{C\varepsilon\|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}\|}{\sigma}

\displaystyle+\frac{L}{c^{\ast}}\|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}\|\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)}\geq 0\bigg\},

where
C=1+\frac{2}{c^{\ast}}
and
L
is a sufficiently large absolute constant. We further require that
c^{\ast}>2
. Set
\sigma^{\prime}=4C\sigma/(1-4/{c^{\ast}}^{2})
. Then
{\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}}(\sigma)\lesssim\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma^{\prime})
.
If
\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma)\gtrsim\sigma
for a sufficiently big constant, we have
\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma)\lesssim{\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}}(\sigma)
. If
\overline{\varepsilon}(\sigma^{\prime})\lesssim\sigma
, then
{\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}}(\sigma)\asymp\sigma\wedge d
.

The next corollary applies Theorem
2.18
to obtain an elegant characterization of LSE optimality.

Corollary 2.19
.

Fix
\sigma
. Then the LSE is minimax optimal if and only if the map
\mu\mapsto w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)
is
(\varepsilon/\sigma)
-Lipschitz up to constants for all
\varepsilon\gtrsim\varepsilon^{\ast}(\sigma)
.

Remark 2.20
.

Nguyen and Khanh (2020)
demonstrate that to show a convex (or concave) function is Lipschitz, it suffices to show it is locally Lipschitz near boundary points. Combined with Corollary
2.19
, we obtain a potentially easier way to verify the Lipschitz property of the mapping
\mu\mapsto w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)
and thus the optimality of the LSE.

3
Examples

In this section, we consider several examples in order to illustrate the utility of the theory we laid out in Section
2
. While some of the examples we consider are well known, the techniques we use to obtain these results are mostly distinct from existing techniques. In addition, we also exhibit many new results including counterexamples to Corollary
2.6
and Proposition
2.4
, and new classes of examples where the LSE is suboptimal. We will repeatedly apply the following useful result from
Yang and Barron (1999)
to study the local entropy.

Lemma 3.1

(
(
Yang and Barron, 1999
, Lemma 3)
)
.

For any set
K
we have,

\displaystyle\log\frac{\mathcal{M}(\tfrac{\varepsilon}{c^{*}},K)}{\mathcal{M}(\varepsilon,K)}\leq\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}_{K}(\varepsilon)\leq\log\mathcal{M}(\tfrac{\varepsilon}{c^{*}},K).

(3.1)

3.1
Examples with optimal LSE

We begin with several examples where the LSE is (nearly) minimax optimal.

3.1.1
Isotonic regression with known total variation bound

In this section, we consider the isotonic estimator with known total variation bound. The case with unknown total variation bound, i.e.,
S^{\uparrow}=\{\mu:\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}\leq\dots\leq\mu_{n}\}
, was essentially analyzed by
Chatterjee (2014)
and he showed in his equation (51) that for any
\mu\in S^{\uparrow}
, we have

w_{S^{\uparrow},\mu}(\varepsilon)\leq C\sqrt{\max(\mu_{n}-\mu_{1},1)\varepsilon n^{1/2}}+\varepsilon^{2}/4.

Set
V=\mu_{n}-\mu_{1}
. Using Proposition
2.9
with
\sigma=1
, the worst case rate of the LSE is upper bounded by
\varepsilon^{2}
where
\varepsilon
solves
\sup\{\varepsilon>0:\varepsilon^{2}/2\leq C\sqrt{\max(V,1)\varepsilon n^{1/2}}+\varepsilon^{2}/4\}
, which is attained by
\varepsilon\asymp n^{1/6}(V\vee 1)^{1/3}
.

We now consider the case with known total variation bound. Consider the set

S_{V}^{\uparrow}:=\{\mu:\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}\leq\ldots\leq\mu_{n},\mu_{n}-\mu_{1}\leq V\},

for some
V\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Importantly, we assume the value of
V
or some appropriate upper bound is known to the statistician so that she can fit the LSE on the set
S_{V}^{\uparrow}
.
We will need to calculate the local entropy of the set
S_{V}^{\uparrow}
. Take any point
\mu^{*}\in S_{V}^{\uparrow}
. We need to pack at a distance
\varepsilon/c
the set
S_{\mu^{*}}^{\uparrow}(V):=\{\mu:\mu\in S_{V}^{\uparrow},\|\mu-\mu^{*}\|\leq\varepsilon\}
. Pick
\mu\in S_{\mu^{*}}^{\uparrow}(V)
. Write
\mu_{1}-\mu_{1}^{*}=:\delta
and suppose that
\delta<-V
. Using this assumption along with the fact that
\mu^{\ast},\mu\in S_{V}^{\uparrow}
, for any
i
, we have

\mu_{i}^{\ast}\geq\mu_{1}^{*}=\mu_{1}-\delta>\mu_{1}+V\geq\mu_{n}\geq\mu_{i}\geq\mu_{1}.

Hence for each
i
,

\displaystyle\mu_{i}^{\ast}-\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mu_{i}^{\ast}-\mu_{1}^{\ast}}_{\geq 0}+\underbrace{\mu_{1}^{\ast}-\mu_{1}}_{=-\delta}+\underbrace{\mu_{1}-\mu_{i}}_{\geq-V}

\displaystyle\geq-\delta-V>0.

Thus,

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}\geq\sum_{i\in[n]}(\mu_{i}-\mu_{i}^{*})^{2}\geq n(-\delta-V)^{2}.

Therefore
-\delta\leq V+\varepsilon/\sqrt{n}
, i.e.,
\delta\geq-V-\varepsilon/\sqrt{n}
. On the contrary, if our assumption that
\delta<-V
does not hold, we have
\delta\geq-V\geq-V-\varepsilon/\sqrt{n}.
So in either case,
\delta\geq-V-\varepsilon/\sqrt{n}
.

Hence
\mu_{1}^{*}-\mu_{1}=-\delta\leq V+\varepsilon/\sqrt{n}
.
A similar argument shows that
\mu_{n}\leq\mu_{n}^{*}+V+\varepsilon/\sqrt{n}
. Thus

\mu_{n}-\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{n}^{*}-\mu_{1}^{*}+2V+\frac{2\varepsilon}{\sqrt{n}}\leq 3V+\frac{2\varepsilon}{\sqrt{n}}.

By
Chatterjee (2014, Lemma 4.20)
we therefore have that

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{M}(\varepsilon/c^{*},S_{\mu^{*}}^{\uparrow}(V))\lesssim\frac{(V\sqrt{n}+\varepsilon)}{(\varepsilon/c^{*})}\asymp\frac{V\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon}.

(3.2)

Since the above holds for any
\mu^{*}
it is an upper bound on the local entropy of
S_{V}^{\uparrow}
by (
3.1
).

Next we will show a lower bound on
\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}_{S_{V}^{\uparrow}}(\varepsilon)
. To this end, we define the set
\mathcal{S}^{\uparrow}(a,b)=\{\mu:a\leq\mu_{1}\leq\ldots\leq\mu_{n}\leq b\}
. We are specifically interested in
S^{\uparrow}(0,V)=\{\mu:0\leq\mu_{1}\leq\ldots\leq\mu_{n}\leq V\}
. First, we note that by
Chatterjee (2014, Lemma 4.20)
,
\log\mathcal{M}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{S}^{\uparrow}(a,b))\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{n}(b-a)}{\varepsilon}
. On the other hand, one can show using Varshamov-Gilbert’s bound that
\log\mathcal{M}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{S}^{\uparrow}(a,b))\gtrsim\frac{\sqrt{n}(b-a)}{\varepsilon}
for values of
\varepsilon\gtrsim\frac{(b-a)}{\sqrt{n}}
. Formally we have:

Lemma 3.2
.

We have that
\log\mathcal{M}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{S}^{\uparrow}(a,b))\gtrsim\frac{\sqrt{n}(b-a)}{\varepsilon}
, for
\varepsilon\gtrsim\frac{(b-a)}{\sqrt{n}}
.

Because the metric entropy is non-increasing, for any
\varepsilon\lesssim\tfrac{b-a}{\sqrt{n}}
, we have

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{M}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{S}^{\uparrow}(a,b))

\displaystyle\geq\log\mathcal{M}\left(\frac{b-a}{\sqrt{n}},\mathcal{S}^{\uparrow}(a,b)\right)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\frac{\sqrt{n}(b-a)}{(b-a)/\sqrt{n}}\gtrsim n.

But also
\log\mathcal{M}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{S}^{\uparrow}(a,b))\lesssim n
trivially holds always
(
Wainwright, 2019
, Example 5.8)
. So for all
\varepsilon\lesssim\tfrac{b-a}{\sqrt{n}}
, we have
\log\mathcal{M}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{S}^{\uparrow}(a,b))\asymp n
, while for all
\varepsilon\gtrsim\tfrac{b-a}{\sqrt{n}}
,
\log\mathcal{M}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{S}^{\uparrow}(a,b))\asymp\tfrac{\sqrt{n}(b-a)}{\varepsilon}
. Hence by using bound (
3.1
) one can see that for sufficiently large
c^{*}
,
\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\mathcal{S}^{\uparrow}(a,b)}(\varepsilon)\asymp\frac{\sqrt{n}(b-a)}{\varepsilon}
for
\varepsilon\gtrsim\frac{(b-a)}{\sqrt{n}}
, and
\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\mathcal{S}^{\uparrow}(a,b)}(\varepsilon)\asymp n
for
\varepsilon\lesssim\frac{(b-a)}{\sqrt{n}}
.

Taking
a=0
and
b=V
, it follows that for
\varepsilon\gtrsim V/\sqrt{n}
, using
S^{\uparrow}(0,V)\subset S_{V}^{\uparrow}
, we have
\sqrt{n}V/\varepsilon\lesssim\mathcal{M}_{S^{\uparrow}(0,V)}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)\leq\mathcal{M}_{S_{V}^{\uparrow}}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)
, and we already argued from (
3.2
) that
\mathcal{M}_{S_{V}^{\uparrow}}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)\lesssim\sqrt{n}V/\varepsilon
. Hence
\mathcal{M}_{S_{V}^{\uparrow}}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)\asymp\sqrt{n}V/\varepsilon
for
\varepsilon\gtrsim V/\sqrt{n}
.

Let us apply Theorem
2.11
to upper bound
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}(\sigma)
. Consider the function

\Psi\colon\varepsilon\mapsto\sup_{\delta\leq\varepsilon}\delta\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\mathcal{S}^{\uparrow}_{V}}(\delta)},

noting that
\Psi(\varepsilon)\lesssim\min(\sqrt{\varepsilon\sqrt{n}V},\sqrt{n}\varepsilon)
for values of
\varepsilon\geq V/\sqrt{n}
using (
3.2
) and the trivial bound of
n
. Equating this to
\varepsilon^{2}/(2\sigma)
, we obtain
\varepsilon\asymp n^{1/6}V^{1/3}\sigma^{2/3}\wedge\sqrt{n}\sigma
. This matches the minimax rate that one can obtain from the equation
\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}_{S_{V}^{\uparrow}}(\varepsilon)\asymp\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}
for
\varepsilon\geq V/\sqrt{n}
.

Thus we conclude that the LSE is minimax optimal (up to logarithmic factors). We note that it is known that the LSE is exactly minimax optimal (see
Zhang (2002)
;
Bellec (2018)
for upper bounds and
Bellec and Tsybakov (2015, Corollary 5)
for lower bounds).

3.1.2
Multivariate Isotonic Regression

We now demonstrate the known result that the LSE for multivariate isotonic regression (with known total variation) is optimal up to logarithmic factors. We will use a combination of existing work from
Han et al. (2019)
on the Gaussian width,
Gao and Wellner (2007)
on packing numbers, and Remark
2.8
.

Let us introduce the set-up from
Han et al. (2019)
. Let
\mathbb{L}_{p,n}=\prod_{j=1}^{p}\{\frac{1}{n^{1/p}},\frac{2}{n^{1/p}},\dots,1\}
be the lattice with
(n^{1/p})^{p}=n
points in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
. For
p=1
, these are just the equispaced points from
1/n
to
1
(inclusive) of distance
n^{-1}
apart. We will consider
p
to be a fixed constant that does not scale with
n
.

Let

\mathcal{F}_{p}=\{f\colon[0,1]^{p}\to[0,1],f\text{ non-decreasing in each variable}\},

where by non-decreasing in each variable we mean that

\displaystyle f(z_{1},\dots,z_{i-1},z_{i},z_{i+1},\dots,z_{p})

\displaystyle\leq f(z_{1},\dots,z_{i-1},z_{i}+y_{i},z_{i+1},\dots,z_{p})

for any
(z_{1},\dots,z_{p})\in[0,1]^{p}
,
y_{i}>0
, and
1\leq i\leq p
. We are interested in the set of evaluations of monotone function on the lattice, i.e.,

\displaystyle Q_{a,b}=\{(f(l_{1}),f(l_{2}),\dots,f(l_{n}))\mid

\displaystyle\qquad f\colon[0,1]^{p}\to[a,b]\text{ non-decreasing in each variable}\}

where
l_{1},\dots,l_{n}
are the distinct elements of
\mathbb{L}_{p,n}
. For
a=0
and
b=1
,
Q_{0,1}
is the set of tuples of evaluations of functions in
\mathcal{F}_{p}
.

Let us compute an upper bound on the log metric entropy of
Q_{a,b}
by using the log-entropy of
\mathcal{F}_{p}
, as is done in
Chatterjee (2014, Lemma 4.20)
in a univariate case. We are using the packing numbers which are up to absolute constants of the same order as the covering numbers used in
Chatterjee (2014)
and
Gao and Wellner (2007)
. Here we use the
L^{2}
-norm with Lebesgue measure in
p
-dimensions in the metric entropy calculations for
\mathcal{F}_{p}
.

Lemma 3.3

(
(
Gao and Wellner, 2007
, Theorem 1.1)
)
.

There is an absolute constant
C
such that if
p>2
, we have

\log\mathcal{M}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F}_{p})\leq C\varepsilon^{-2(p-1)}.

If
p=2
, then

\log\mathcal{M}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F}_{p})\leq C\varepsilon^{-2}(\log 1/\varepsilon)^{2}.

Using this result, we will obtain the following:

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
Q_{a,b}
be the set of evaluations of monotone functions on the lattice defined above. Then for
p>2
, we have

\log\mathcal{M}(\varepsilon,Q_{a,b})\leq C\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2\sqrt{n}(b-a)}\right)^{-2(p-1)}

for some absolute constant
C
. For
p=2
, we have

\log\mathcal{M}(\varepsilon,Q_{a,b})\leq C\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2\sqrt{n}(b-a)}\right)^{-2}\left(\log\frac{2\sqrt{n}(b-a)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}.

The proof is broadly similar to
Chatterjee (2014)
except instead of constructing monotone step functions on the real-line, we construct a multivariate analogue on
[0,1]^{p}
. We will upper bound the cardinality of a minimal
\varepsilon
-covering of
Q_{a,b}
, which therefore bounds the cardinality of a maximal
\varepsilon
-packing of
Q_{a,b}
.

Next, we derive in Lemma
3.5
a lower bound on
\log\mathcal{M}(\varepsilon,Q_{a,b})
following the technique in
Gao and Wellner (2007, Proposition 2.1)
. The proof involves partitioning
[0,1]^{p}
into cubes of side length
\varepsilon
and constructing many piece-wise monotone functions whose values disagree with each other on sufficiently many cubes (equivalently, binary strings in some dimension that have sufficiently large Hamming distances between each other).

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
Q_{a,b}
be the set of evaluations of monotone functions on the lattice defined above. Then for
p\geq 2
, if
\frac{\varepsilon}{b-a}\gtrsim\sqrt{n/n^{1/p}}
, we have
\log\mathcal{M}\left(\varepsilon,Q_{a,b}\right)\gtrsim\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2\sqrt{n}(b-a)}\right)^{-2(p-1)}.

To summarize the results of these two lemmas, when
\frac{\varepsilon}{b-a}\gtrsim\sqrt{n/n^{1/p}}
, we have for
p\geq 2
that

\displaystyle\left(\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2\sqrt{n}(b-a)}\right)^{-2(p-1)}

(3.3)

\displaystyle\lesssim\log\mathcal{M}\left(\varepsilon,Q_{a,b}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\begin{cases}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2\sqrt{n}(b-a)}\right)^{-2}\Big(\log\frac{2\sqrt{n}(b-a)}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{2},&p=2,\\
\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2\sqrt{n}(b-a)}\right)^{-2(p-1)},&p>2.\end{cases}

In the following lemma, we use the bound (
3.1
) to derive the local metric entropy from the global entropy results when
p>2
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Suppose
\frac{\varepsilon}{b-a}\gtrsim\sqrt{n/n^{1/p}}
and
p>2
. Then for sufficiently large
c^{\ast}
,
\log\mathcal{M}_{Q_{a,b}}^{\operatorname{loc}}\left(\varepsilon\right)\asymp\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2\sqrt{n}(b-a)}\right)^{-2(p-1)}
.

Using this result, we show that the least squares estimator achieves the minimax rate up to logarithmic factors. Consider the set
Q_{a,b}
where
a=-b=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}
, and take
\sigma\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}
.
Then we have for
\frac{1}{n^{1/2p}}\lesssim\varepsilon\lesssim 1
(noting that the packing set has cardinality 1 when
\varepsilon
is larger than the diameter) and
p>2
that

\log\mathcal{M}_{Q_{-1/\sqrt{n},1/\sqrt{n}}}^{\operatorname{loc}}\left(\varepsilon\right)\asymp\varepsilon^{-2(p-1)}.

(3.4)

Lemma 3.7
.

Suppose
p>2
. Take
1/\sqrt{n}\leq\sigma\leq 1
. Then minimax rate
\varepsilon^{\ast}=\sup\{\varepsilon:\varepsilon^{2}/\sigma^{2}\leq\log\mathcal{M}_{Q_{-1/\sqrt{n},1/\sqrt{n}}}^{\operatorname{loc}}\left(\varepsilon\right)\}
satisfies
\varepsilon^{\ast}\asymp\sigma^{1/p}
. If
\sigma=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}
, then the LSE rate satisfies
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}\lesssim\sigma^{1/p}\log^{2}(n).

Thus, we have shown for
p>2
the LSE achieves the minimax rate up to a logarithmic factor when
\sigma=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}
, as already demonstrated in
Han et al. (2019)
. We later show in Section
3.2.2
that if
\sigma>\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}
, the LSE is suboptimal for some values of
\sigma
. In an improvement to the LSE,
Deng and Zhang (2020)
propose a computationally-tractable block estimator that actually achieves the minimax rate for a range of
\sigma
without the logarithmic factor under some moment conditions on the noise term.

3.1.3
Hyperrectangle Example

We will first argue that the LSE is optimal on any hyperrectangle. This appears to be a folklore fact, but since we could not find a reference containing a proof we attach an argument for completeness. Since it is hard to evaluate the local widths for arbitrary hyperrectangles we use other means. First observe that without loss of generality we may assume that the hyperrectangle is axis aligned. This is because the LSE is invariant to shifts and rotations. Let
H:=\prod_{i=1}^{n}[-a_{i}/2,a_{i}/2]
for some
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, where we assume without loss of generality that
a_{1}\leq\ldots\leq a_{n}
.

First observe that the minimization
\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-\nu_{i})^{2}
subject to
\nu\in H
splits into minimizations
\hat{\nu}_{i}:=\min_{\nu_{i}\in[-a_{i}/2,a_{i}/2]}(Y_{i}-\nu_{i})^{2}
. In the one dimensional case with convex set
K=[-a_{i}/2,a_{i}/2]
, the estimator defined by
Neykov (2022)
coincides with
\hat{\nu}_{i}
(see Lemma
D.1
). It follows from
Neykov (2022, Corollary III.1)
(see also
Donoho et al. (1990)
) that
\mathbb{E}(\hat{\nu}_{i}-\mu_{i})^{2}\asymp\min(a_{i}^{2},\sigma^{2})
, a fact which is also easy to directly verify. Thus the LSE has risk

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|\hat{\nu}-\mu\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}(\hat{\nu}_{i}-\mu_{i})^{2}\asymp\sum_{i=1}^{n}\min(a_{i}^{2},\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle\asymp[(k+2)\sigma^{2}\wedge\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{2}],

(3.5)

where
k\in\{0,n-1\}
is such that
(k+1)\sigma^{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n-k}a_{i}^{2}
and
(k+2)\sigma^{2}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n-k-1}a_{i}^{2}
. The last implication is proved in the following lemma. But
Neykov (2022, Section III.A)
establishes
[(k+2)\sigma^{2}\wedge\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{2}]
as the minimax rate in
n
dimensions, proving optimality of the LSE. Note that we did not directly apply Corollary III.1 to the
n
-dimensional optimization since we would have to establish the estimator from
Neykov (2022)
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
coincides with the LSE, which may not be true.

Lemma 3.8
.

We have
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\min(a_{i}^{2},\sigma^{2})\asymp[(k+2)\sigma^{2}\wedge\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{2}]
where
k
is as defined above.

Consider now the hyperrectangle
H
defined by taking
a_{i}=1/\sqrt{n}
for
1\leq i\leq n-1
and
a_{n}=C\gg 1
. Let
c^{\ast}>1/2
be the sufficiently large absolute constant in
Neykov (2022)
and then consider the Gaussian width of
B(0,2c^{\ast})\cap H
, i.e.,
w_{H,0}(2c^{\ast})=\mathbb{E}\sup_{x\in H,\|x\|\leq 2c^{*}}\langle x,\xi\rangle
. Taking
x_{i}=\mathop{\mathrm{sign}}(\xi_{i})/(2\sqrt{n})
, we see that the width is
\gtrsim\sqrt{n}
. On the other hand, consider packing
B(0,2c^{\ast})\cap H
at a distance
2c^{*}/c^{*}=2
, i.e., computing
\mathcal{M}_{H}^{\operatorname{loc}}(2c^{\ast})
. Observe that the intersection of the ball and
H
is fully contained in a hyperrectangle
H^{\prime}
of side lengths
n^{-1/2}\times n^{-1/2}\times\cdots n^{-1/2}\times 4c^{*}
, so
\mathcal{M}_{H}^{\operatorname{loc}}(2c^{\ast})\leq\mathcal{M}_{H^{\prime}}^{\operatorname{loc}}(2c^{\ast})
. Partition
H^{\prime}
into hypercubes of side length
1/\sqrt{n}
along the long edge of
H^{\prime}
, with possibly a region left-over of diameter
\leq 1
. We can fit at most
\lceil 4c^{*}/(1/\sqrt{n})\rceil\leq 4c^{*}\sqrt{n}+1
hypercubes inside
H^{\prime}
. Note that each of these hypercubes of side length
1/\sqrt{n}
can have at most
1
point from the packing set since its diameter is bounded by
1
. This means
\log\mathcal{M}_{H}^{\operatorname{loc}}(2c^{\ast})\leq\log\mathcal{M}_{H^{\prime}}^{\operatorname{loc}}(2c^{\ast})\lesssim\log n
. Thus
w_{H,0}(2c^{\ast})/2c^{\ast}\gtrsim\sqrt{n}\gg\sqrt{\log n}\gtrsim\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}_{H}^{\operatorname{loc}}(2c^{\ast})}
, noting that
2c^{\ast}
is smaller than the diameter since we take
C\gg 1
. Thus, the sufficient condition from Corollary
2.6
is not necessary for LSE optimality.

The same example can serve to show that the condition of Proposition
2.4
is only a sufficient condition. Suppose
a_{n}=C\geq\sqrt[4]{n}
and
\sigma=1
. Since for
\varepsilon
of constant order the width is at least proportional to
\sqrt{n}
, we can show
\bar{\varepsilon}\gtrsim\sqrt[4]{n}
. To see this, take
\varepsilon\asymp\sqrt[4]{n}
, so

\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2\sigma}\asymp\sqrt{n}\lesssim w_{H,0}(1)\leq w_{H,0}(\varepsilon)\leq\sup_{\mu\in H}w_{H,\mu}(\varepsilon).

Then,
\overline{\varepsilon}\gtrsim\varepsilon\asymp\sqrt[4]{n}
by definition as a supremum. Since
d\geq a_{n}\geq\sqrt[4]{n}
, we have
\overline{\varepsilon}\wedge d\gtrsim\sqrt[4]{n}
. Hence Proposition
2.4
gives a prediction which is far away from the true LSE rate which is constant in this case, using (
3.5
).

3.1.4
Subspace (Linear Regression)

Suppose we are given a linear regression model
Y=X\beta+\varepsilon
, where
\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I}_{p})
and
X
is an arbitrary fixed design matrix
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
. This is a special case of our setting where we can consider
K=\operatorname{col}(X)
to be a fixed
p
-dimensional subspace in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. It is very simple to see that
w_{\mu}(\varepsilon)=w_{\nu}(\varepsilon)
for any
\mu,\nu\in K
, so that the LSE will be minimax optimal by Corollary
2.19
(or Theorem
2.15
). This is a significant albeit well-known result: for any fixed design
X
, the least squares procedure (i.e., linear regression) is optimal in terms of in-sample squared prediction error as long as
p\leq n
. By in-sample prediction error, we mean
\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon}\|X(\hat{\beta}-\beta)\|_{2}^{2}
, where we assume
\mu=X\beta\in\operatorname{col}(X)
, and the least squares estimate
\hat{\mu}\in\operatorname{col}(X)
is
\hat{\mu}=X\hat{\beta}
where
\hat{\beta}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\|Y-X\gamma\|_{2}^{2}
. To see how this result can be derived using other means, one can consult Exercise 13.2, Example 13.8, and Example 15.14 in
Wainwright (2019)
.

3.1.5
\ell_{1}
ball and
\ell_{2}
balls: LSE is optimal

Let
K=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\|x\|_{1}\leq 1\}
be the
\ell_{1}
ball. Then
w_{K,0}(\varepsilon)\asymp\sqrt{\log(en(\varepsilon^{2}\wedge 1))}\wedge\varepsilon\sqrt{n}
as stated in
Bellec (2019)
. The following lemma states the local entropy and is proven by combining Lemma
3.1
along with the global entropy.

Lemma 3.9
.

The local metric entropy of the
\ell_{1}
ball satisfies

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)\asymp\begin{cases}\frac{\log(\varepsilon^{2}n)}{\varepsilon^{2}}&\varepsilon\gtrsim 1/\sqrt{n}\\
n&\varepsilon\lesssim 1/\sqrt{n}\text{ or }\varepsilon\asymp 1/\sqrt{n},\end{cases}

provided
c^{\ast}
is taken sufficiently large.

We now apply Corollary
2.6
by verifying that
w_{K,0}(\varepsilon)/\varepsilon\lesssim\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)}
using Lemma
3.9
. When
\varepsilon\gtrsim 1/\sqrt{n}
,

\displaystyle\frac{w_{K,0}(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\asymp\sqrt{n}\wedge\frac{\sqrt{\log(en(\varepsilon^{2}\wedge 1))}}{\varepsilon}\lesssim\sqrt{n}\wedge\frac{\sqrt{\log(n\varepsilon^{2})}}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\log(n\varepsilon^{2})}}{\varepsilon}\asymp\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)}.

When
\varepsilon\lesssim 1/\sqrt{n}
,

\displaystyle\frac{w_{K,0}(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\asymp\sqrt{n}\wedge\frac{\sqrt{\log(en(\varepsilon^{2}\wedge 1))}}{\varepsilon}\lesssim\sqrt{n}

\displaystyle\asymp\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)}.

Thus the LSE is minimax optimal for all
\sigma
.

Next, we let
K=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\|x\|_{2}\leq 1\}=B(0,1)
be the
\ell_{2}
ball. We consider
\varepsilon<1
. Observe from
Vershynin (2018, Proposition 7.5.2(vi))
that

\displaystyle\frac{w_{K,0}(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\frac{w(B(0,\varepsilon)\cap K)}{\varepsilon}=\frac{w(B(0,\varepsilon))}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon\cdot\sqrt{n}}{\varepsilon}=\sqrt{n}.

Then using scaling properties of the metric entropy of a set along with
Vershynin (2018, Corollary 4.2.13)
,

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{M}(\varepsilon/c^{\ast},B(0,\varepsilon)\cap K)

\displaystyle=\log\mathcal{M}(\varepsilon/c^{\ast},B(0,\varepsilon))

\displaystyle=\log\mathcal{M}\left(1/c^{\ast},B(0,1)\right)

\displaystyle\gtrsim n\log c^{\ast}.

Thus,
\frac{w_{K,0}(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}\lesssim\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}(\varepsilon/c^{\ast},B(0,\varepsilon)\cap K)}
. Hence
by Corollary
2.6
(using the symmetry of
K
) the LSE is minimax optimal for all
\sigma
.

3.2
Examples with suboptimal LSE

Here we attach several examples to illustrate the suboptimality of the LSE. Examples of this nature have previously appeared in
Chatterjee (2014)
;
Zhang (2013)
. Below, when we say that the LSE is suboptimal, we mean that there exist values of
\sigma
for which the LSE does not achieve the minimax optimal rate. It is easy to see (Proposition
3.10
) that the LSE is always optimal for either very small or large values of
\sigma
.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
r
be the largest radius of a ball fully embedded in
K
. Then the LSE is minimax optimal if either
\sigma\lesssim r/\sqrt{n}
or
\sigma\gtrsim d
.

Proof.

First assume
\sigma\lesssim r/\sqrt{n}
. Suppose the ball with maximal radius
r
is centered at
\mu\in K
.
Take
\varepsilon=\sqrt{n}\sigma\lesssim r
. Then
\varepsilon^{2}/\sigma^{2}=n
, while

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(r)\geq\log\mathcal{M}(r/c^{\ast},B(\mu,r)\cap K)

\displaystyle=\log\mathcal{M}(r/c^{\ast},B(\mu,r))\gtrsim n,

following the argument we used in Section
3.1.5
for the
\ell_{2}
ball. Hence
\varepsilon^{2}/\sigma^{2}\lesssim\log\mathcal{M}_{K}^{\operatorname{loc}}(\varepsilon)
, so
\varepsilon^{\ast}\gtrsim\varepsilon=\sqrt{n}\sigma
by (
1.2
). But
\sqrt{n}\sigma
is the rate for the trivial estimator that just returns
Y
(see also the proof of Corollary
2.12
). The LSE is also always better than the trivial estimator (projections onto convex sets move closer to any point in the set), so
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}\lesssim\sqrt{n}\sigma
. Thus, the LSE is minimax optimal for
\sigma\lesssim r/\sqrt{n}
.

Lastly, assume
\sigma\gtrsim d
. By Lemma
1.4
,
\varepsilon^{\ast}\gtrsim\sigma\wedge d\asymp d
but also
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}\leq d
as desired.
∎

3.2.1
Pyramid Example

Assume
\sigma=1
. Let
v
be an orthogonal vector to the convex set
K\subset v^{\perp}
, and assume
w(K)\geq\mathrm{diam}^{2}(K)\geq 1
, and
\|v\|_{2}\geq\mathrm{diam}(K)+c
. Assume further that
K
is symmetric (i.e., if
k\in K
,
-k\in K
). Consider the set
P=\cup_{\alpha\in[0,1]}[\alpha v+(1-\alpha)K]
. It is simple to see
P
is a convex set, in fact a pyramid, using the symmetry assumption.

Lemma 3.11
.

For sufficiently large
c
and assuming
w(K)\gtrsim\|v\|_{2}^{2}
for a sufficiently large constant, the worst case LSE risk
\varepsilon_{P,\mathrm{LS}}^{2}
satisfies
\varepsilon_{P,\mathrm{LS}}\gtrsim\|v\|_{2}
.

On the other hand, there exists a simple estimator that achieves a better rate of convergence. Suppose
Y=p+\xi
where
p=\alpha v+(1-\alpha)k\in P
and
\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I}_{n})
. Consider the linear projection
P_{v}Y
of
Y
onto
v
(i.e.,
P_{v}=vv^{T}/\|v\|^{2}
), so that
P_{v}Y=\alpha v+P_{v}\xi
using orthogonality of
v
to
K
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|P_{v}Y-p\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\|\alpha v+P_{v}\xi-(\alpha v+(1-\alpha)k)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\|P_{v}\xi-(1-\alpha)k\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\|P_{v}\xi\|_{2}^{2}+(1-\alpha)^{2}\mathrm{diam}(K)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\mathrm{diam}(K)^{2}\ll\|v\|^{2}_{2}.

Note that we used
\sup_{k\in K}\|k\|_{2}^{2}\leq\mathrm{diam}(K)^{2}
, which holds since
0\in\operatorname{relint}K
.

3.2.2
Multivariate Isotonic Regression with
\sigma>1/\sqrt{n}

We return to the multivariate isotonic regression setting, where now we take
\sigma>1/\sqrt{n}
and
p>2
and show the LSE is suboptimal. Our result relies on analyzing the local Gaussian width at the 0 point (Lemma
D.2
and Lemma
D.3
in the appendix) by mimicking the proof of
Han et al. (2019, Proposition 5)
. The authors consider a more general set than
Q_{a,b}
, though, with no restrictions on the range.

Lemma 3.12
.

Set
K=Q_{-1/\sqrt{n},1/\sqrt{n}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and set
p>2
. Then for

\displaystyle\sigma\in\left(\max\left\{n^{-\frac{1}{2}},n^{\frac{2-p}{2p-2}}(\log n)^{\frac{4p}{p-1}}\right\},n^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{p}}\right),

(3.6)

the LSE rate
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}^{2}
exceeds the minimax rate
\sigma^{2/p}
.

Proof.

Recall from Section
3.1.2
that the minimax rate is given by
\sigma^{2/p}
when
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\lesssim\sigma\lesssim 1
. Now assume
\sigma
satisfies (
3.6
), assuming momentarily that the given interval is a non-empty interval. This clearly satisfies
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\lesssim\sigma\lesssim 1
condition.

Next, using Lemma
D.2
and Lemma
D.3
and taking
c=\frac{1}{\log^{4}n}\in(0,1)
, we conclude
w_{0}(t)-w_{0}(ct)\gtrsim n^{1/2-1/p}t
for
t\in(0,1)
. Since
\sigma\leq n^{-1/2+1/p}
, we have
n^{1/2-1/p}\sigma<1
. Set
t\asymp n^{1/2-1/p}\sigma
so that

w_{0}(t)-w_{0}(ct)\geq\frac{t^{2}}{\sigma}\geq\frac{t^{2}(1-c^{2})}{\sigma}.

Rearranging,

w_{0}(t)-\frac{t^{2}}{\sigma}\geq w_{0}(ct)-\frac{t^{2}c^{2}}{\sigma},

which by the concavity of
t\mapsto w_{0}(t)-t^{2}/\sigma
implies that
\varepsilon_{0,w}(\sigma)\geq ct
(where
\varepsilon_{0,w}
is defined as in (
2.1
)). Thus, since
\sigma>1/\sqrt{n}
, it follows that

\varepsilon_{0,w}\gtrsim ct\asymp cn^{1/2-1/p}\sigma>\sigma.

The last inequality follows for sufficiently large
n
since
n^{1/2-1/p}\gg\log^{4}n=c^{-1}
.

Then, by Lemma
2.1
,
\mathbb{E}\|\hat{\mu}-0\|^{2}\asymp\varepsilon_{0,w}^{2}
, which implies
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}\gtrsim\varepsilon_{0,w}
. Since we showed
\varepsilon_{0,w}^{2}\gtrsim c^{2}t^{2}
, we have
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}^{2}\gtrsim c^{2}n^{1-2/p}\sigma^{2}
. This quantity is
\gg\sigma^{2/p}
whenever
\sigma\gg n^{(2-p)/(2p-2)}(\log n)^{4p/(p-1)}
, as we required. Thus, the LSE is suboptimal for such
\sigma
.

It remains to check that the interval in (
3.6
) is non-empty. One can verify that

n^{\frac{2-p}{2p-2}}(\log n)^{\frac{4p}{p-1}}\ll n^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{p}},

since this is equivalent to
(\log n)^{\beta_{1}}\ll n^{\beta_{2}}
where
\beta_{1}=\frac{4p}{p-1}>0
and
\beta_{2}=\frac{p-2}{2p(p-1)}>0
for
p>2
. Moreover, clearly
n^{-1/2}\ll n^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{p}}
. Thus we have a non-trivial range for
\sigma
with a suboptimal LSE.
∎

3.2.3
Solid of Revolution

Let
f\colon[0,b]\to\mathbb{R}
be concave, satisfy
f(0)=f(b)=0
, and satisfy
f(x)=f(b-x)
for
x\leq b/2
(i.e., symmetric about
b/2
). We assume
b/4\gg f(b/2)
and that

\displaystyle f(b/2)>f(b/4)+\tfrac{b}{4\sqrt{2\pi(n-1)}},

(3.7)

i.e., the secant line between the points at
x=b/4
and
x=b/2
has slope at least
\tfrac{1}{8\sqrt{2\pi(n-1)}}
. To define the solid of revolution in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, for any
x\in[0,b]
, define
B_{x}
as the the
(n-1)
-dimensional ball with radius
f(x)
and centered at the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
. Then let
K=\bigcup_{x}(\{x\}\times B_{x})
.

Lemma 3.13
.

The worst case LSE risk for the solid of revolution
K
satisfies
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}\gtrsim b
.

On the other hand, given
y=\mu+\xi
where
\mu\in K
, consider the estimator
\hat{y}=\langle y,e_{1}\rangle e_{1}
that projects
y
onto the
x
-axis. Then decomposing
\mu=\langle\mu,e_{1}\rangle e_{1}+(\mu-\langle\mu,e_{1}\rangle e_{1})
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mu-\langle y,e_{1}\rangle e_{1}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|(\langle\mu,e_{1}\rangle-\langle y,e_{1}\rangle)e_{1}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\mu-\langle\mu,e_{1}\rangle e_{1}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\langle\xi,e_{1}\rangle e_{1}\|_{2}^{2}+[f(b/2)]^{2}

\displaystyle=\langle\xi,e_{1}\rangle^{2}+[f(b/2)]^{2}.

The second inequality came from noting that
\mu-\langle\mu,e_{1}\rangle e_{1}
lies in
e_{1}^{\perp}\cap K
, and any such point is no more than distance
f(b/2)
from the
x
-axis. So in expectation, we have
\mathbb{E}\|\mu-\langle y,e_{1}\rangle e_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 1+[f(b/2)]^{2}\ll 1+b^{2}/8.
Thus,
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}^{2}\gtrsim b^{2}
while
\mathbb{E}\|\mu-\hat{y}\|_{2}^{2}\ll 1+b^{2}
.

3.2.4
Prelude to Ellipsoids

In this section, we derive a lower bound on the LSE rate for a broad class of sets
K
that are pre-images of smooth functions, which in turn are transformations of Minkowski gauges. Later, we specialize our result to the case of ellipsoids.

Suppose we are given a convex set of the type
K=\{x:G(x)\leq 1\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
G:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is a twice continuously differentiable non-negative convex function with
G(0)=0
. Suppose that the Hessian of
G
admits a lower bound on
K
, i.e., for all
x\in K
we have
H(x)=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial yy{{}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}}G(y)\big|_{y=x}\succeq M\succ 0
, for some symmetric and strictly positive-definite matrix
M
(so that
G
is strongly convex). Take any boundary point
x\in\operatorname{bd}K
, i.e., such that
G(x)=1
. We know that
\nabla G(x){{}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}x\geq\nabla G(x){{}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}y
for all
y\in K
, or in other words, the gradient
\nabla G(x)
is an outward normal to
K
. However, under our assumptions above, we actually know more about the set
K
. We know that

\displaystyle G(y)

\displaystyle=G(x)+\nabla G(x){{}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}(y-x)+\frac{(y-x){{}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}H(\tilde{x})(y-x)}{2}

\displaystyle\geq G(x)+\nabla G(x){{}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}(y-x)+\frac{(x-y){{}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}M(x-y)}{2},

(3.8)

where
\tilde{x}=\alpha x+(1-\alpha)y
for some
\alpha\in[0,1]
. Since
x
is a boundary point and
y\in K
,
G(y)-G(x)\leq 0
, hence (
3.8
) implies

\displaystyle\nabla G(x){{}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}(x-y)\geq(x-y){{}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}M(x-y)/2

(3.9)

for all
y\in K
. We will also argue that under these assumptions that the set
K
is compact. It is clear by the continuity of
G
that
K
is closed. For boundedness, pick
y\in K
and observe

\displaystyle 1\geq G(y)\geq G(0)+\nabla G(0){{}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}(y)+y{{}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}My/2.

But, since
0
is a point of minimum, we have
G(0)=0
and
\nabla G(0)=0
, so that
y^{T}My/2\leq G(y)\leq 1
. Thus, since
M
is strictly positive-definite, the set
K
is bounded. Denote by
d
the finite diameter of
K
.

Suppose we now select the boundary point with the smallest gradient, i.e., let
x\in\operatorname{bd}K
be a point such that
\|\nabla G(x)\|_{2}
is minimized. This smallest gradient will not be a zero vector since

0=G(0)\geq G(x)-\nabla G(x){{}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}x=1-\nabla G(x){{}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}x.

Applying (
3.9
), there exists some outward normal vector at
x
given by
x^{*}=\frac{\nabla G(x)}{\|\nabla G(x)\|_{2}}
such that for all
y\in K
we have

\displaystyle\langle x^{*},x-y\rangle\geq(x-y){{}^{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle T}}}\tilde{M}(x-y)/2,

(3.10)

where
\tilde{M}=M/\|\nabla G(x)\|_{2}
.

A lower bound on
\varepsilon_{K,w}(\sigma)
for suitable
\sigma
is given in the following bound using these vectors
x
and
x^{\ast}
, where recall
\varepsilon_{K,w}(\sigma)
is the supremum over
\mu\in K
of the term
\varepsilon_{\mu,w}(\sigma)
defined in (
2.1
).

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})
be the vector of eigenvalues of
\tilde{M}/2
. Given the above assumptions,
\varepsilon_{K,w}(\sigma)\gtrsim w^{2}(K)/\sum(1/\lambda_{i})
for
\sigma\gtrsim d^{2}/(w(K))
.

3.2.5
A necessary condition for optimality on ellipsoids

We now specialize the scenario in the previous section to ellipsoids. That is, suppose we are given
K=\{x:\|Dx\|^{2}_{2}\leq 1\}
with
D
is a diagonal positive-definite matrix, i.e., we take
G(x)=\|Dx\|^{2}_{2}
in the notation of Section
3.2.4
. Let the diagonal entries of
D
be given by
d_{1}\geq d_{2}\geq\ldots\geq d_{n}
. We again derive a lower bound on
\varepsilon_{K,w}(\sigma)
and obtain a necessary condition for optimality of the LSE similar to the sufficient condition in Corollary
2.6
.

Lemma 3.15
.

Suppose we have the ellipsoid
K=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\|Dx\|_{2}^{2}\leq 1\}
where
D
is a positive definite diagonal matrix with entries
d_{1}\geq d_{2}\geq\ldots\geq d_{n}
. Define
\delta_{n-k}=1/d_{n-k}
for any
k\in\{0\}\cup[n-1]
. Then for each such
k
, we have
\varepsilon_{K,w}(\sigma)\gtrsim\delta_{n-k}
when
\sigma\gtrsim\delta_{n-k}^{2}/w_{0}(\delta_{n-k})
for a sufficiently large absolute constant. Moreover, if the LSE is minimax optimal for
K
for all
\sigma
, then for all
k\in\{0\}\cup[n-1]
we must have

\displaystyle w_{0}(\delta_{n-k})\lesssim\delta_{n-k}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{loc}}(c\delta_{n-k})},

for sufficiently large absolute constant and sufficiently small
c>0
.

Let us now construct some examples of ellipsoids with a suboptimal LSE using this lemma. Take
\delta=1/d_{n}\asymp\mathrm{diam}(K)
. By Lemma
3.15
we know that for values of
\sigma=1/(d_{n}^{2}\sqrt{\sum 1/d_{i}^{2}})
we have that
\varepsilon_{K,w}(\sigma)\gtrsim\delta
, where we used
Wainwright (2019, Exercise 5.9)
to write
w_{0}(\delta)=w(K)\asymp\sqrt{\sum 1/d_{i}^{2}}
. Note that

\displaystyle\delta\gtrsim\sigma=1\Big/\penalty\left(d_{n}^{2}\sqrt{\sum 1/d_{i}^{2}}\right).

Then for some
\nu\in K
,
\varepsilon_{\nu,w}(\sigma)\gtrsim\delta\gtrsim\sigma
, so by Lemma
2.1
,

\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}(\sigma)\geq\mathbb{E}\|\hat{\mu}-\nu\|^{2}\asymp\varepsilon_{\nu,w}(\sigma)\gtrsim\delta\asymp\mathrm{diam}(K).

But since
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}(\sigma)\lesssim\mathrm{diam}(K)
always holds, this means
\varepsilon_{K,\mathrm{LS}}(\sigma)\asymp\delta\asymp\mathrm{diam}(K)
. On the other hand, the minimax rate for ellipsoids, stated in
Neykov (2022)
(where in his notation
a_{i}=1/d_{i}^{2}
and
d_{0}=\infty
), is given by
(k+1)\sigma^{2}\wedge\mathrm{diam}(K)^{2}
if
1/d_{n-k}^{2}\leq(k+1)\sigma^{2}
but
1/d_{n-k+1}^{2}>k\sigma^{2}
, and is
\mathrm{diam}(K)^{2}
if
1/d_{n}^{2}\leq\sigma^{2}
.

Let us ensure that we choose
k
and the
d_{i}
so that the minimax rate is given by
(k+1)\sigma^{2}
with
k=1
. It is clear that
\sigma<1/d_{n}
. Suppose now that
d_{1}/\sqrt{n}\ll d_{n}\ll d_{n-1}
and in addition
1/d_{n-1}^{2}\leq 2\sigma^{2}
which is equivalent to
\sum\frac{1}{d_{i}^{2}}\leq\frac{2d_{n-1}^{2}}{d_{n}^{4}}
. Then
k=1
satisfies the stated conditions in the minimax rate in the first scenario. Note that
d_{1}/\sqrt{n}\ll d_{n}
implies
2\sigma^{2}\ll 1/d_{n}^{2}\asymp\mathrm{diam}(K)^{2}\asymp\delta^{2}
. It follows that the minimax rate would be at most
\frac{2}{d_{n}^{4}\sum 1/d_{i}^{2}}=2\sigma^{2}\ll\delta^{2}
, while we showed
\delta^{2}
is the worst case rate for the LSE.

One can construct multiple such examples. One example is given in
Zhang (2013)
with
d_{i}=1
for
i<n
and
d_{n}=1/\sqrt[4]{n}
, where the lower bound on the worst-case rate of the LSE is established with a bare hands argument. Then the minimax rate is
2\sigma^{2}\asymp 1
for this example, while the worst-case rate for the LSE is
